6.4  Planning as a CSP
To  run  the  demo,  in  folder  "aipython",  load  "stripsCSPPlanner.py",
and  copy  and  paste  the  commented-out  example  queries  at  the  bot-
tom of that file. This assumes Python 3.
The CSP planner assumes there is a single action at each step. This creates a
CSP that can use any of the CSP algorithms to solve (e.g., stochastic local search
or arc consistency with domain splitting).
It uses the same action representation as before;  it does not consider fac-
tored actions (action features), or implement state constraints.
stripsCSPPlanner.py — CSP planner where actions are represented using STRIPS
11  from cspProblem import Variable, CSP, Constraint
12
13  class CSP_from_STRIPS(CSP):
14      """A CSP where:
15      * CSP variables are constructed for each feature and time, and each
action and time
16      * the dynamics are specified by the STRIPS representation of actions
17      """
18
19      def __init__(self, planning_problem, number_stages=2):
20         prob_domain = planning_problem.prob_domain
21         initial_state = planning_problem.initial_state
22         goal = planning_problem.goal
23         # self.action_vars[t] is the action variable for time t
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

6.4.  Planning as a CSP145
24         self.action_vars = [Variable(f"Action{t}", prob_domain.actions)
25                               for t in range(number_stages)]
26         # feat_time_var[f][t] is the variable for feature f at time t
27         feat_time_var = {feat: [Variable(f"{feat}_{t}",dom)
28                                       for t in range(number_stages+1)]
29                          for (feat,dom) in
prob_domain.feature_domain_dict.items()}
30
31         # initial state constraints:
32         constraints = [Constraint([feat_time_var[feat][0]], is_(val),
33                                    f"{feat}[0]={val}")
34                           for (feat,val) in initial_state.items()]
35
36         # goal constraints on the final state:
37         constraints += [Constraint([feat_time_var[feat][number_stages]],
38                                      is_(val),
f"{feat}[{number_stages}]={val}")
39                           for (feat,val) in goal.items()]
40
41         # precondition constraints:
42         constraints += [Constraint([feat_time_var[feat][t],
self.action_vars[t]],
43                                  if_(val,act),
44                                  f"{feat}[{t}]={val} if action[{t}]={act}")
45                           for act in prob_domain.actions
46                           for (feat,val) in act.preconds.items()
47                           for t in range(number_stages)]
48
49         # effect constraints:
50         constraints += [Constraint([feat_time_var[feat][t+1],
self.action_vars[t]],
51                                  if_(val,act),
52                                  f"{feat}[{t+1}]={val} if action[{t}]={act}")
53                           for act in prob_domain.actions
54                           for feat,val in act.effects.items()
55                           for t in range(number_stages)]
56         # frame constraints:
57
58         constraints += [Constraint([feat_time_var[feat][t],
self.action_vars[t], feat_time_var[feat][t+1]],
59                                  eq_if_not_in_({act for act in
prob_domain.actions
60                                               if feat in act.effects}),
61                               f"{feat}[t]={feat}[{t+1}] if act not in
{set(act for act in prob_domain.actions
if feat in act.effects)}")
62                           for feat in prob_domain.feature_domain_dict
63                           for t in range(number_stages) ]

65                                          for feat in

prob_domain.feature_domain_dict
66                                          for t in range(number_stages+1)}

68
69      def extract_plan(self,soln):
70         return [soln[a] for a in self.action_vars]
The following methods return methods which can be applied to the particular
environment.
For example, is_(3) returns a function that when applied to 3, returns True
and when applied to any other value returns False. So is_(3)(3) returns True
and is_(3)(7) returns False.
Note that the underscore (’_’) is part of the name; we use the convention
that  a  function  with  name  ending  in  underscore  returns  a  function.   Com-
mented out is an alternative style to define is_ and if_; returning a function
defined by lambda is equivalent to returning the embedded function,  except
that the embedded function has a name.  The embedded function can also be
given a docstring.
stripsCSPPlanner.py — (continued)
72  def is_(val):
73      """returns a function that is true when it is it applied to val.
74      """
75      #return lambda x: x == val
76      def is_fun(x):
77         return x == val
78      is_fun.__name__ = f"value_is_{val}"
79      return is_fun
80
81  def if_(v1,v2):
82      """if the second argument is v2, the first argument must be v1"""
83      #return lambda x1,x2: x1==v1 if x2==v2 else True
84      def if_fun(x1,x2):
85         return x1==v1 if x2==v2 else True
86      if_fun.__name__ = f"if x2 is {v2} then x1 is {v1}"
87      return if_fun
88
89  def eq_if_not_in_(actset):
90      """first and third arguments are equal if action is not in actset"""
91      # return lambda x1, a, x2: x1==x2 if a not in actset else True
92      def eq_if_not_fun(x1, a, x2):
93         return x1==x2 if a not in actset else True
94      eq_if_not_fun.__name__ = f"first and third arguments are equal if
action is not in {actset}"
95      return eq_if_not_fun
Putting it together, this returns a list of actions that solves the problem for
a given horizon. If you want to do more than just return the list of actions, you
might want to get it to return the solution.  Or even enumerate the solutions
(by using Search_with_AC_from_CSP).
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

99      """

101      sol = Con_solver(csp).solve_one()

6.5  Partial-Order Planning
To run the demo, in folder "aipython", load "stripsPOP.py", and copy
and paste the commented-out example queries at the bottom of that
file.
A partial order planner maintains a partial order of action instances.  An
action instance consists  of a name  and  an index.   You need action  instances
because the same action could be carried out at different times.
stripsPOP.py — Partial-order Planner using STRIPS representation
11  from searchProblem import Arc, Search_problem
12  import random
13
14  class Action_instance(object):
15      next_index = 0
16      def __init__(self,action,index=None):
17         if index is None:
18             index = Action_instance.next_index
19             Action_instance.next_index += 1
20         self.action = action
21         self.index = index
22
23      def __str__(self):
24         return f"{self.action}#{self.index}"
25
26      __repr__ = __str__ # __repr__ function is the same as the __str__
function
A  partial-order  planner  is  represented  as  a  search  problem  (Section  3.1)
where a node consists of:
• actions: a set of action instances.
• constraints:  a set of (a
1
, a
2
) pairs, where a
1
and a
2
are action instances,
which represents that a
1
must come before a
2
in the partial order.  There
are a number of ways that this could be represented. The code below rep-
resents the set of pairs that are in transitive closure of the before relation.
This lets it quickly determine whether some before relation is consistent
with the current constraints, at the cost of pre-computing and storing the
transitive closure.
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

6.5.  Partial-Order Planning149
•  agenda:  a list of (s, a) pairs, where s is a (var, val) pair and a is an action
instance.  This means that variable var must have value val before a can
occur.
•  causal_links: a set of (a0, g, a1) triples, where a
1
and a
2
are action instances
and g is a (var, val) pair. This holds when action a
0
makes g true for action
a
1
.
stripsPOP.py — (continued)
28  class POP_node(object):
29      """a (partial) partial-order plan. This is a node in the search
space."""
30      def __init__(self, actions, constraints, agenda, causal_links):
31         """
32         * actions is a set of action instances
33         * constraints a set of (a0,a1) pairs, representing a0<a1,
34           closed under transitivity
35         * agenda list of (subgoal,action) pairs to be achieved, where
36           subgoal is a (variable,value) pair
37         * causal_links is a set of (a0,g,a1) triples,
38           where ai are action instances, and g is a (variable,value) pair
39         """
40         self.actions = actions # a set of action instances
41         self.constraints = constraints # a set of (a0,a1) pairs
42         self.agenda = agenda  # list of (subgoal,action) pairs to be
achieved
43         self.causal_links = causal_links # set of (a0,g,a1) triples
44
45      def __str__(self):
46         return ("actions: "+str({str(a) for a in self.actions})+
47                "\nconstraints: "+
48                str({(str(a1),str(a2)) for (a1,a2) in self.constraints})+
49                "\nagenda: "+
50                str([(str(s),str(a)) for (s,a) in self.agenda])+
51                "\ncausal_links:"+
52                str({(str(a0),str(g),str(a2)) for (a0,g,a2) in
self.causal_links}) )
extract_plan constructs a total order of action instances that is consistent
with the partial order.
stripsPOP.py — (continued)
54      def extract_plan(self):
55         """returns a total ordering of the action instances consistent
56         with the constraints.
57         raises IndexError if there is no choice.
58         """
59         sorted_acts = []
60         other_acts = set(self.actions)
61         while other_acts:
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

1506.  Deterministic Planning
62             a = random.choice([a for a in other_acts if
63                     all(((a1,a) not in self.constraints) for a1 in
other_acts)])
64             sorted_acts.append(a)
65             other_acts.remove(a)
66         return sorted_acts
POP_search_from_STRIPS  is  an  instance  of  a  search  problem.   As  such,  it
needs start nodes, a goal, and the neighbors function.

77      def is_goal(self, node):
78         return node.agenda == []

80      def start_node(self):
81         constraints = {(self.start, self.finish)}
82         agenda = [(g, self.finish) for g in
self.planning_problem.goal.items()]
83         return POP_node([self.start,self.finish], constraints, agenda, [] )

Given a causal link (a0, subgoal, a1), the following method protects the causal
link from each action in actions. Whenever an action deletes subgoal, the action
needs to be before a0 or after a1.  This method enumerates all constraints that
result from protecting the causal link from all actions.
stripsPOP.py — (continued)
120      def protect_cl_for_actions(self, actions, constrs, clink):
121         """yields constraints that extend constrs and
122         protect causal link (a0, subgoal, a1)
123         for each action in actions
124         """
125         if actions:
126             a = actions[0]
127             rem_actions = actions[1:]
128             a0, subgoal, a1 = clink
129             if a != a0 and a != a1 and self.deletes(a,subgoal):
130                if self.possible((a,a0),constrs):
131                    new_const = self.add_constraint((a,a0),constrs)
132                    for e in
self.protect_cl_for_actions(rem_actions,new_const,clink):
yield e # could be "yield from"
133                if self.possible((a1,a),constrs):
134                    new_const = self.add_constraint((a1,a),constrs)
135                    for e in

Given an action act,  the following method protects all the causal links in
clinks from act. Whenever act deletes subgoal from some causal link (a0, subgoal, a1),
the action act needs to be before a0 or after a1. This method enumerates all con-
straints that result from protecting the causal links from act.
stripsPOP.py — (continued)
141      def protect_all_cls(self, clinks, act, constrs):
142         """yields constraints that protect all causal links from act"""
143         if clinks:
144             (a0,cond,a1) = clinks[0] # select a causal link
145             rem_clinks = clinks[1:] # remaining causal links
146             if act != a0 and act != a1 and self.deletes(act,cond):
147                if self.possible((act,a0),constrs):
148                    new_const = self.add_constraint((act,a0),constrs)
149                    for e in self.protect_all_cls(rem_clinks,act,new_const):
yield e
150                if self.possible((a1,act),constrs):
151                    new_const = self.add_constraint((a1,act),constrs)
152                    for e in self.protect_all_cls(rem_clinks,act,new_const):
yield e
153             else:
154                for e in self.protect_all_cls(rem_clinks,act,constrs): yield
e
155         else:
156             yield constrs

158      def achieves(self,action,subgoal):
159         var,val = subgoal
160         return var in self.effects(action) and self.effects(action)[var] ==
val
161
162      def deletes(self,action,subgoal):
163         var,val = subgoal
164         return var in self.effects(action) and self.effects(action)[var] !=
val
165

167         """returns the variable:value dictionary of the effects of action.
168         works for both actions and action instances"""

170             action = action.action

The constraints are represented as a set of pairs closed under transitivity.
Thus if (a, b) and (b, c) are the list, then (a, c) must also be in the list. This means
that adding a new constraint means adding the implied pairs,  but querying
whether some order is consistent is quick.
stripsPOP.py — (continued)
178      def add_constraint(self, pair, const):
179         if pair in const:
180             return const
181         todo = [pair]
182         newconst = const.copy()
183         while todo:
184             x0,x1 = todo.pop()
185             newconst.add((x0,x1))
186             for x,y in newconst:
187                if x==x1 and (x0,y) not in newconst:
188                    todo.append((x0,y))
189                if y==x0 and (x,x1) not in newconst:
190                    todo.append((x,x1))
191         return newconst
192
193      def possible(self,pair,constraint):
194         (x,y) = pair
195         return (y,x) not in constraint

Chapter 7
Supervised Machine Learning
This first chapter on machine learning covers the following topics:
•  Data: how to load it, splitting into training, validation and test sets
•  Features:  many of the features come directly from the data.  Sometimes
it is useful to construct features, e.g.  height>  1.9m might be a Boolean
feature constructed from the real-valued feature height.
•  Learning with no input features:  this is the base case of many methods.
What should you predict if you have no input features?  This provides
the  base  cases  for  many  algorithms  (e.g.,  decision  tree  algorithm)  and
baselines that more sophisticated algorithms need to beat.
•  Decision  tree  learning:   one  of  the  classic  and  simplest  learning  algo-
rithms, which is the basis of many other algorithms.
•  Cross validation and parameter tuning: methods to prevent overfitting.
•  Linear regression and classification:  other classic and simple techniques
that often work well (particularly combined with feature learning or en-
gineering).
•  Boosting: combining simpler learning methods to make even better learn-
ers.
A  good  source  of  classic  datasets  is  the  UCI  Machine  Learning  Repository
https://archive.ics.uci.edu/datasets [Lichman, 2013] [Dua and Graff, 2017].
The  SPECT,  IRIS,  and  car  datasets  (carbool  is  a  Boolean  version  of  the  car
dataset) are from this repository.
One  source  of  multiple  datasets  is  Movielens  (described  in  Section  7.8),
which provides ratings of hundreds of thousands of people on tens of thousand
155

7.1  Representations of Data and Predictions
The code uses the following definitions and conventions:
•  A dataset contains examples.
•  An example is a list (or tuple) of values.  The values can be numbers or
strings.
•  A feature is a function from examples into the range of the feature. Each
feature f has the following attributes:
f.ftype, the type of f, one of: "boolean", "categorical", "numeric"
f.frange, the set of values of f seen in the dataset, represented as a list.
The ftype is inferred from the frange if not given explicitly.
f.__doc__, the docstring, a string description of f (for printing).
A Boolean feature is a function from the examples into{False, True}. So,
if f  is a Boolean feature, f .frange == [False, True], and if e is an example,
f(e) is either True or False.
A categorical feature has a number of discrete values as the range.   A
numeric feature has real numbers or integers as the range.

7.1.  Representations of Data and Predictions157
A dataset is consists of training examples (train), validation examples (valid)
and test examples (test).   It can automatically partition examples into these
sets.   The  target  feature  is  a  feature  that  a  learner  making  a  prediction  of.
Dataset ds has the following attributes:
ds.train a list of training examples
ds.valid a list of validation examples
ds.test a list of test examples
ds.target_index the index of the target
ds.target the feature corresponding to the target
ds.input_features a list of input features

89             ftype = self.infer_type(frange)
90             if one_hot and ftype == "categorical" and ind !=
self.target_index:
91                if len(frange) == 2:
92                    # two values, the feature is equality to one of them.
93                    true_val = list(frange)[1] # choose one as true
94                    def feat(e, i=ind, tv=true_val):
95                        return e[i]==tv
96                    if self.header:
97                        feat.__doc__ = f"{self.header[ind]}=={true_val}"
98                    else:
99                        feat.__doc__ = f"e[{ind}]=={true_val}"
100                    feat.frange = boolean
101                    feat.ftype = "boolean"
102                    self.input_features.append(feat)
103                else:
104                    for val in frange:
105                        def feat(e, i=ind, v=val):
106                           return e[i]==v
107                        if self.header:
108                           feat.__doc__ = self.header[ind]+"="+val
109                        else:
110                           feat.__doc__ = f"e[{ind}]={val}"
111                        feat.frange = boolean
112                        feat.ftype = "boolean"
113                        self.input_features.append(feat)
114             else:
115                def feat(e,index=ind):
116                  return e[index]
117                if self.header:
118                    feat.__doc__ = self.header[ind]
119                else:
120                    feat.__doc__ = f"e[{ind}]"
121                feat.frange = frange
122                feat.ftype = ftype
123                if ind == self.target_index:
124                    self.target = feat
125                else:
126                    self.input_features.append(feat)
The following infers the type of each feature. Sometimes this can be wrong
(e.g., when numbers are really categorories), and the type of a feature may need
to be set explicitly, by assigning ftype (and perhaps frange).

129         """Infers the type of a feature with domain
130         """

131         if all(v in {True,False} for v in domain) or all(v in {0,1} for v
in domain):
132             return "boolean"
133         if all(isinstance(v,(float,int)) for v in domain):
134             return "numeric"
135         else:
136             return "categorical"
7.1.1  Creating Boolean Conditions from Features
Some algorithms require Boolean input features (features with range {0, 1}).
In order to be able to use these algorithms on datasets with arbitrary domains
of input variables, the following code constructs Boolean conditions from the
attributes.
There are 3 cases:
•  If the feature is already Boolean, use it.
•  When the values are all numeric, assume they are ordered (as opposed
to just being some classes that happen to be labelled with numbers) and
construct Boolean features for splits of the data.   That is,  the feature is
e[ind]< cut for some value cut.  The number of cut values is less than or
equal to max_num_cuts.
•  When the values are not all numeric, it creates an indicator function for
each value. An indicator function for a value returns true when that value
is given and false otherwise. Note that you can’t create an indicator func-
tion for values that appear in the test set but not in the training or valida-
tion sets because training doesn’t have access to the test set. An example
in the test set with a value for a feature that doesn’t appear in the training
set, has all indicator functions returning false.
learnProblem.py — (continued)
138      def conditions(self, max_num_cuts=7):
139         """returns a list of boolean conditions from the input features
140         max_num_cuts: maximum number of cuts for numeric features
141         """
142         conds = []
143         for feat in self.input_features:
144             if feat.ftype =='boolean':
145                conds.append(feat)
146             elif all(isinstance(val,(int,float)) for val in feat.frange):
147                # all numeric, create cuts of the data
148                sorted_frange = sorted(feat.frange)
149                num_cuts = min(max_num_cuts,len(feat.frange))
150                cut_positions = [len(feat.frange)*i//num_cuts for i in
range(1,num_cuts)]
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

7.1.  Representations of Data and Predictions161
151                for cut in cut_positions:
152                    cutat = sorted_frange[cut]
153                    def featv(e, ft=feat, cutat=cutat):
154                        return ft(e) < cutat
155                    featv.__doc__ = f"{feat.__doc__}<{cutat}"
156                    featv.frange = boolean
157                    featv.ftype = "boolean"
158                    conds.append(featv)
159             else:
160                # create an indicator function for every value
161                for val in frange:
162                    def featv(e, v=val):
163                       return feat(e) == v
164                    featv.__doc__ = f"{feat.__doc__}=={val}"
165                    featv.frange = boolean
166                    featv.ftype = "boolean"
167                    conds.append(featv)
168         return conds
Exercise 7.1Change  the  code  so  that  it  splits  using  e[ind] ≤  cut  instead  of
e[ind]<  cut.   Check boundary cases,  such as 3 elements with 2 cuts.   As a test
case, make sure that when the range is the 30 integers from 100 to 129, with 2 cuts,
the resulting Boolean features should be e[ind] ≤ 109 and e[ind] ≤ 119 so each of
the resulting domains is of equal size.
Exercise 7.2   This splits on whether the feature is less than one of the values in
the training set.  Sam suggested it might be better to split between the values in
the training set, and suggested using
cutat = (sorted_frange[cut] + sorted_frange[cut− 1])/2
Why might Sam have suggested this?   Does this work better?   (Try it on a few
numeric datasets).
Exercise 7.3   Instead of comparing to a single value, is it better to have ranges?
That is, if adjacent split conditions are a and b, where a< b instead of having less
than a and less than b (when less than b implies less than a) have “between a and
b” is the condition. Make sure to check boundary conditions.
7.1.2  Evaluating Predictions
A predictor is a function that takes an example and makes a prediction on the
values of the target features.
A loss takes a prediction and the actual value and returns a non-negative
real number; lower is better (except for accuracy, which is 1 minus the 0-1 loss).
The error for a dataset the mean loss.
The function evaluate_dataset returns the average loss for each example,
where the loss for each example depends on the evaluation criteria.
learnProblem.py — (continued)
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

1627.  Supervised Machine Learning
170      def evaluate_dataset(self, data, predictor, loss):
171         """Evaluates predictor on data according to the loss
172         predictor is a function that takes an example and returns a
173                prediction for the target features.
174         loss(prediction,actual) -> non-negative real
175         """
176         if data:
177             try:
178                value = statistics.mean(loss(predictor(e), self.target(e))
179                                       for e in data)
180             except ValueError: # if loss gives an error
181                return float("inf") # infinity
182             return value
183         else:
184             return math.nan # not a number
Three  losses  are  implemented:  the  squared  or  L2  loss  (the  square  of  the
difference between the actual and predicted values), absolute or L1 loss (the
absolute difference between the actual and predicted values) and the log loss
(the negative log-likelihood, base 2, which can be interpreted as the number
of  bits  to  describe  an  example  using  a  code  based  on  the  prediction  treated
as a probability).  The 0-1 (zero-one) loss is the count of number of times the
prediction is wrong. The accuracy is also defined, but it is not a loss as it should
be maximized.
Thee are defined using the class Loss, but no instances will be created. Just
use Loss.squared etc.  (Please keep the __doc__ strings a consistent length as
they  are  used  in  tables.)   The  prediction  is  either  a  real  value  or  a {value  :
probability} dictionary or a list.   The actual is either a real number or a cate-
gory (which can be used as the key of a dictionary prediction).
learnProblem.py — (continued)
186  class Loss(object):
187      """A container for the loss measures"""
188
189      def squared(prediction, actual):
190         "squared loss "
191         if isinstance(prediction, (list,dict)):
192             expected_val = sum(v*p for (v,p) in prediction.items())
193             # return (1-prediction[actual])**2 # the correct value is 1
194             return (expected_val - actual)**2
195         else:
196              return (prediction-actual)**2
197
198      def absolute(prediction, actual):
199         "absolute loss "
200         if isinstance(prediction, (list,dict)):
201              return abs(1-prediction[actual]) # the correct value is 1
202         else:
203             return abs(prediction-actual)
204
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

7.1.  Representations of Data and Predictions163
205      def log(prediction, actual):
206         "log loss (bits)"
207         try:
208             if isinstance(prediction, (list,dict)):
209                 return -math.log2(prediction[actual])
210             else:
211                return -math.log2(prediction) if actual==1 else
-math.log2(1-prediction)
212         except ValueError:
213             return float("inf") # infinity
214
215      def zero_one(prediction, actual):
216         "zero_one     "
217         return themode(prediction) != actual
218
219      def accuracy(prediction, actual):
220         "accuracy     "
221         return 1-Loss.zero_one(prediction, actual)
222
223      all_criteria = [accuracy, absolute, squared, log]
224
225  def themode(prediction):
226      """the mode of a prediction. Used for accuracy.
227      This handles all of the cases of AIPython predictors: dictionaries,
lists and real predictions (for integer/booleans).
228      """
229      if isinstance(prediction, dict):
230         md, val = None, -math.inf
231         for (p,v) in prediction.items():
232             if v> val:
233                md, val = p,v
234         return md
235      if isinstance(prediction, list):
236         md,val = 0,prediction[0]
237         for i in range(1,len(prediction)):
238             if prediction[i]>val:
239                md,val = i,prediction[i]
240         return md
241      else: # prediction is real number; round to nearest integer
242         return round(prediction)
7.1.3  Creating Test and Training Sets
The following partitions the data into a training set and a test set. (Also training
into training and validation sets). This does not guarantee that the test set will
contain exactly a proportion of the data equal to prob_test.
[An alternative is to use random.sample() which can guarantee that the test
set will contain exactly a particular proportion of the data. However this would
require knowing how many elements are in the dataset, which it may not know,
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

1647.  Supervised Machine Learning
as data may just be a generator of the data (e.g., when reading the data from a
file).]
learnProblem.py — (continued)
244  def partition_data(data, prob_test=0.30):
245      """partitions the data into a training set and a test set, where
246      prob_test is the probability of each example being in the test set.
247      """
248      train = []
249      test = []
250      for example in data:
251         if random.random() < prob_test:
252             test.append(example)
253         else:
254             train.append(example)
255      return train, test
7.1.4  Importing Data From File
A dataset is typically loaded from a file. The default here is that it loaded from
a CSV (comma separated values) file, although the separator can be changed.
All  lines  that  contain  the  separator  are  assumed  to  be  valid  data  (so  it  only
includes those data items that contain more than one element). This allows for
blank lines and comment lines that do not contain the separator.  However, it
means that this method is not suitable for cases where there is only one feature.
Note that data_all and data_tuples are generators.  data_all is a generator of
lists of strings.  This version assumes that CSV files are simple.  The standard
csv package, that allows quoted arguments, can be used by uncommenting the
line for data_all and commenting out the line that follows. data_tuples contains
only those lines that contain the delimiter (others lines are assumed to be empty
or comments), and tries to convert the elements to numbers whenever possible.
learnProblem.py — (continued)

7.1.5  Augmented Features
Sometimes you may want to augment the features with new features computed
from the old features (e.g., the product of features). The following code creates
a new dataset from an old dataset but with new features.  The product of fea-
tures is special cases of these are kernels.  Some algorithms (e.g.  support vector
machines) exploit kernels much more efficiently than the methods below.
Recall that a feature is a function of examples. A unary feature constructor
takes a feature and returns a new feature. A binary feature combiner takes two
features and returns a new feature.
learnProblem.py — (continued)
347  class Data_set_augmented(Data_set):
348      def __init__(self, dataset, unary_functions=[], binary_functions=[],
include_orig=True):
349         """creates a dataset like dataset but with new features
350         unary_function is a list of unary feature constructors
351         binary_functions is a list of binary feature combiners.
352         include_orig specifies whether the original features should be
included
353         """
354         self.orig_dataset = dataset
355         self.unary_functions = unary_functions
356         self.binary_functions = binary_functions
357         self.include_orig = include_orig
358         self.target = dataset.target
359         Data_set.__init__(self,dataset.train, test=dataset.test,
360                         target_index = dataset.target_index)
361
362      def create_features(self, one_hot=False):
363         """create the set of features.
364            one_hot is ignored, but could be implemented as in
Data_set.create_features
365         """
366         if self.include_orig:
367             self.input_features = self.orig_dataset.input_features.copy()
368         else:
369             self.input_features = []
370         for u in self.unary_functions:
371             for f in self.orig_dataset.input_features:
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

1687.  Supervised Machine Learning
372                self.input_features.append(u(f))
373         for b in self.binary_functions:
374             for f1 in self.orig_dataset.input_features:
375                for f2 in self.orig_dataset.input_features:
376                    if f1 != f2:
377                        self.input_features.append(b(f1,f2))
The following are useful unary feature constructors and binary feature com-
biner.
learnProblem.py — (continued)
379  def square(f):
380      """a unary feature constructor to construct the square of a feature
381      """
382      def sq(e):
383         return f(e)**2
384      sq.__doc__ = f.__doc__+"**2"
385      sq.frange=f.frange
386      sq.ftype=f.ftype
387      return sq
388
389  def power_feat(n):
390      """given n returns a unary feature constructor to construct the nth
power of a feature.
391      e.g., power_feat(2) is the same as square, defined above
392      """
393      def fn(f,n=n):
394         def pow(e,n=n):
395             return f(e)**n
396         pow.__doc__ = f"{f.__doc__}**{n}"
397         pow.frange=f.frange
398         pow.ftype=f.ftype
399         return pow
400      return fn
401
402  def prod_feat(f1,f2):
403      """a new feature that is the product of features f1 and f2
404      """
405      def feat(e):
406         return f1(e)*f2(e)
407      feat.__doc__ = f1.__doc__+"*"+f2.__doc__
408      feat.frange=f1.frange
409      feat.ftype=f1.ftype
410      return feat
411
412  def eq_feat(f1,f2):
413      """a new feature that is 1 if f1 and f2 give same value
414      """
415      def feat(e):
416         return 1 if f1(e)==f2(e) else 0
417      feat.__doc__ = f1.__doc__+"=="+f2.__doc__
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

7.2.  Generic Learner Interface169
418      feat.frange = boolean
419      feat.ftype = "boolean"
420      return feat
421
422  def neq_feat(f1,f2):
423      """a new feature that is 1 if f1 and f2 give different values.
424      This is xor for Boolean variables and can be used to implement parity
425      """
426      def feat(e):
427         return 1 if f1(e)!=f2(e) else 0
428      feat.__doc__ = f1.__doc__+"!="+f2.__doc__
429      feat.frange = boolean
430      feat.ftype = "boolean"
431      return feat

Exercise 7.4    For symmetric properties,  such as product,  you don’t need both
f 1∗ f 2 as well as f 2∗ f 1 as extra properties.  Allow the user to be able to declare
feature constructors as symmetric (by associating a Boolean feature with them).

7.2  Generic Learner Interface
A learner takes a dataset (and possibly other arguments specific to the method).
To get it to learn, call the learn() method. This implements Displayable (section
1.7.1) so that it can display traces at multiple levels of detail (perhaps with a
GUI).
learnProblem.py — (continued)
439  from display import Displayable
440
441  class Learner(Displayable):
442      title = "Unknown learner"
443      def __init__(self, dataset):
444         raise NotImplementedError("Learner.__init__") # abstract method
445
446      def learn(self):
447         """returns a predictor, a function from a tuple to a value for the
target feature
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

1707.  Supervised Machine Learning
448         """
449         raise NotImplementedError("learn") # abstract method

452         """the learned predictor, available after learn()
453         """
454         raise NotImplementedError("predictor") # abstract method
455

457         """String representation of the learned predictor
458         """

460
461      def evaluate(self):
462         """Evaluates learner on training and test data
463         """
464         print(f"function learned is {self}")
465         print("Criterion\tTraining\tvalidation\ttest")
466         for ecrit in Loss.all_criteria:
467             print(ecrit.__doc__, end='\t')
468             for data_subset in [self.dataset.train, self.dataset.valid,
self.dataset.test]:
469                error = self.dataset.evaluate_dataset(data_subset,
self.predictor, ecrit)
470                print(str(round(error,7)), end='\t')
471             print()
7.3  Learning With No Input Features
If  you  need  make  the  same  prediction  for  each  example  (the  input  features
are ignored), what prediction should you make?  This can be used as a naive
baseline; if a more sophisticated method does not do better than this, it is not
useful. This also provides the base case for some methods, including decision-
tree learning.
To run demo to compare different prediction methods on various eval-
uation  criteria,  in  folder  "aipython",  load  "learnNoInputs.py",  using
e.g., python -i learnNoInputs.py, and it prints some test results.
There are a few alternatives as to what could be allowed in a prediction:
•  a point prediction, where only the values in the domain of the feature can
be predicted. For example, if the values of the feature are{0, 1}, you are
only allowed to predict 0 or 1;  if the values are ratings in {1, 2, 3, 4, 5},
you can only predict one of these values.
•  a point prediction, where any value can be predicted. For example, if the
values of the feature are{0, 1} it could predict 0.3, 1, or even 1.7.  For all
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

7.3.  Learning With No Input Features171
of the criteria defined, there is no point in predicting a value greater than
the maximum value or less that the smallest (but it is allowed).   If the
values are ratings in{1, 2, 3, 4, 5}, a possible prediction is 3.4.
•  a probability distribution over the values of the feature. For each value v,
it predicts a non-negative number p
v
, such that the sum over all predic-
tions is 1.
Here are some prediction functions that take in an enumeration of values,
a  domain,  and  returns  a  point  prediction:  a  value  or  dictionary  of {value  :
prediction}.   Note  that cmedian  returns  one  of  the  middle  values  when  there
are an even number of examples, whereas median gives the average of them
(and so cmedian is applicable for ordinals that cannot be considered cardinal
values). Similarly, cmode picks one of the values when more than one value has
the maximum number of elements.
learnNoInputs.py — Learning ignoring all input features
11  from learnProblem import Loss
12  import math, random, collections, statistics
13  import utilities # argmax for (element,value) pairs
14
15  class Predict(object):
16      """The class of prediction methods for a list of values.
17      The doc strings the same length because they are used in tables.
18      Note that the methods don't have the self argument.
19      To use call Predict.laplace(data) etc."""
20
21      ### The following return a distribution over values (for classification)
22      def empirical(data, domain=[0,1], icount=0):
23         "empirical dist "
24         # returns a distribution over values
25         # icount is pseudo count for each value
26         counts = {v:icount for v in domain}
27         for e in data:
28             counts[e] += 1
29         s = sum(counts.values())
30         return {k:v/s for (k,v) in counts.items()}
31
32      def claplace(data, domain=[0,1]):
33         "Laplace       "  # for categorical data
34         return Predict.empirical(data, domain, icount=1)
35
36      def cmode(data, domain=[0,1]):
37         "mode          " # for categorical data
38         md = statistics.mode(data)
39         return {v: 1 if v==md else 0 for v in domain}
40
41      def cmedian(data, domain=[0,1]):
42         "median        " # for categorical data
43         md = statistics.median_low(data) # always return one of the values
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

1727.  Supervised Machine Learning
44         return {v: 1 if v==md else 0 for v in domain}
45
46      ### The following return a single prediction (for regression).
47      ### The domain argument is ignored.
48
49      def mean(data, domain=[0,1]):
50         "mean          "
51         # returns a real number
52         return statistics.mean(data)
53
54      def rmean(data, domain=[0,1], mean0=0, pseudo_count=1):
55         "regularized mean"
56         # returns a real number.
57         # mean0 is the mean to be used for 0 data points
58         # With mean0=0.5, pseudo_count=2, same as laplace for [0,1] data
59         # this works for enumerations, not just lists
60         sm = mean0 * pseudo_count
61         # return (sm+sum(data))/(pseudo_count+len(data))
62         count = pseudo_count
63         for e in data:
64             sm += e
65             count += 1
66         return sm/count
67
68      def laplace(data, domain=[0,1]):
69         "Laplace       "  # for boolean (or numeric with finite domain, eg
[1..5])
70         return Predict.rmean(data, domain=[0,1],
mean0=statistics.mean(domain), pseudo_count=2)
71
72      def mode(data, domain=[0,1]):
73         "mode          "
74         return statistics.mode(data)
75
76      def median(data, domain=[0,1]):
77         "median        "
78         return statistics.median(data)
79
80      all = [empirical, mean, rmean, laplace, cmode, mode, median, cmedian]
81
82      # The following suggests appropriate predictions as a function of the
target type
83      select = {"boolean": [empirical, laplace, cmode, cmedian],
84               "categorical": [empirical, laplace, cmode, cmedian],
85               "numeric": [mean, rmean, mode, median]}

7.3.1  Evaluation
To evaluate a point prediction, let’s first generate some possible values, 0 and
1 for the target feature.  Given the ground truth prob, a number in the range
[0, 1], the following code generates some training and test data where prob is
the probability of each example being 1. To generate a 1 with probability prob,
it generates a random number in range [0,1] and return 1 if that number is less
than prob.  A prediction is computed by applying the predictor to the training
data, which is evaluated on the test set. This is repeated num_samples times.
Let’s  evaluate  the  predictions  of  the  possible  selections  according  to  the
different evaluation criteria, for various training sizes.

7.4  Naive Bayes
This implementation of naive Bayes assumes all features are categorical (which
includes  Boolean).   It  treats  numerical  features  as  categorical  (assuming  the
values in the training data are the only values).

13  class Naive_Bayes(Learner):
14      title = "Naive Bayes"
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

7.4.  Naive Bayes175
15      def  __init__(self,
16                 dataset,
17                  pseudo = 0):
18         self.dataset = dataset
19         self.pseudo = pseudo
20
21      def learn(self):
22         """learn parameters"""
23         target = self.dataset.target
24
25         # target_counts[t] is count of target=t
26         self.target_counts = {t:self.pseudo for t in target.frange}
27         # counts[i][iv][t] is count of input#i=iv and target=t
28         self.counts = [{v:{t:self.pseudo for t in target.frange}
29                        for v in f.frange}
30                       for f in self.dataset.input_features]
31         for eg in self.dataset.train:
32             self.target_counts[target(eg)] += 1
33             for i,f in enumerate(self.dataset.input_features):
34                self.counts[i][f(eg)][target(eg)] += 1
35         self.target_dist = normalize(self.target_counts)
36         self.cprobs = [{v:normalize(self.counts[i][v])
37                        for v in f.frange}
38                       for i,f in enumerate(self.dataset.input_features)]
39         return self.predictor
40
41      def predictor(self, eg):
42         unnorm = self.target_dist.copy()
43         for i,f in enumerate(self.dataset.input_features):
44             mult_elts(unnorm,self.cprobs[i][f(eg)])
45         return normalize(unnorm)
46
47  def normalize(dct):
48      """normalize the values of a dictionary
49      """
50      sum_counts = sum(dct.values())
51      return {v:n/sum_counts for (v,n) in dct.items()}
52
53  def mult_elts(d1,d2):
54      """multiply corresponding values of two dictionaries with the same keys
55      """
56      return {k:d1[k]*d2[k] for k in d1.keys()}

7.5  Decision Tree Learning
To  run  the  decision  tree  learning  demo,  in  folder  "aipython",  load
"learnDT.py", using e.g., ipython -i learnDT.py, and it prints some
test  results.   To  try  more  examples,  copy  and  paste  the  commented-
out commands at the bottom of that file.  This requires Python 3 with
matplotlib.
The decision tree algorithm does binary splits, and assumes that all input
features are Boolean functions of the examples (as can be constructed using the
conditions method (page 160)).
learnDT.py — Learning a binary decision tree

15  class DT_learner(Learner):

34                                  else Predict.claplace if
dataset.target.ftype=='categorical'
else None)

7.5.  Decision Tree Learning177
35         else:
36             self.leaf_prediction = leaf_prediction
37         self.max_num_cuts = max_num_cuts
38         if gamma is not None:
39             self.gamma = gamma
40         else:
41             self.gamma =
2+math.log2(len(dataset.train)+1)+math.log2(len(dataset.input_features))
42             if self.target.ftype=='numeric' and len(dataset.train)>1: #
when prediction is a real, scale
43                self.gamma = statistics.stdev(dataset.target(e) for e in
dataset.train)*self.gamma

45         if train is None:

47         else:

49         self.num_digits = 3
50
51      def learn(self, max_num_cuts=8):
52         """learn a decision tree"""
53         self.predictor =
self.learn_tree(self.dataset.conditions(self.max_num_cuts),
self.train)
54         return self.predictor
55
56      def __str__(self):
57        """string only exists after learning"""
58        return self.predictor.__doc__
The main recursive algorithm, takes in a set of input features and a set of
training data. It first decides whether to split. If it doesn’t split, it makes a point
prediction, ignoring the input features.
It only splits if the best split decreases sum of the errors by at least gamma.
This implies it does not split when:
•  there are no more input features
•  there are fewer examples than min_number_examples
•  all the examples agree on the value of the target, or
•  the best split puts all examples in the same partition.
If it splits, it selects the best greedy (assuming that it only gets to do one)
split according to the evaluation criterion, and returns the condition to split on
(in the variable split) and the corresponding partition of the examples.
learnDT.py — (continued)
60      def learn_tree(self, conditions, data_subset):
61         """returns a decision tree
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

1787.  Supervised Machine Learning
62         conditions is a set of possible conditions
63         data_subset is a subset of the data used to build this (sub)tree
64
65         where a decision tree is a function that takes an example and
66         makes a prediction on the target feature
67         """
68         self.display(2,f"learn_tree with {len(conditions)} features and
{len(data_subset)} examples")
69         split, partn = self.select_split(conditions, data_subset)
70         if split is None: # no split; return a point prediction
71             prediction = self.leaf_value(data_subset, self.target.frange)
72             self.display(2,f"leaf prediction for {len(data_subset)}
examples is {prediction}")
73             def leaf_fun(e):
74                return prediction
75             leaf_fun.__doc__ = str(prediction) if isinstance(prediction,
dict) else f"{prediction:.{self.num_digits}f}"
76             leaf_fun.num_leaves = 1
77             return leaf_fun
78         else:  # a split succeeded
79             false_examples, true_examples = partn
80             rem_features = [fe for fe in conditions if fe != split]
81             self.display(2,"Splitting on",split.__doc__,"with examples
split",
82                          len(true_examples),":",len(false_examples))
83             true_tree = self.learn_tree(rem_features,true_examples)
84             false_tree = self.learn_tree(rem_features,false_examples)
85             def fun(e):
86                if split(e):
87                    return true_tree(e)
88                else:
89                    return false_tree(e)
90             #fun = lambda e: true_tree(e) if split(e) else false_tree(e)
91             fun.__doc__ = (f"(if {split.__doc__} then {true_tree.__doc__}"
92                          f" else {false_tree.__doc__})")
93             fun.num_leaves = true_tree.num_leaves + false_tree.num_leaves
94             return fun
learnDT.py — (continued)
96      def leaf_value(self, egs, domain):
97         return self.leaf_prediction((self.target(e) for e in egs), domain)
98
99      def select_split(self, conditions, data_subset):
100         """finds best feature to split on.
101
102         conditions is a non-empty list of features.
103         returns feature, partition
104         where feature is an input feature with the smallest error as
105               judged by loss or
106               feature==None if there are no splits that improve the error
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

7.5.  Decision Tree Learning179
107         partition is a pair (false_examples, true_examples) if feature is
not None
108         """
109         best_feat = None # best feature
110         best_error = self.sum_losses(data_subset) - self.gamma
111         self.display(3,f" no split.
prediction:{self.leaf_value(data_subset, self.target.frange)},
error:{best_error+self.gamma}, to beat:{best_error}")
112         best_partition = None
113         for feat in conditions:
114             false_examples, true_examples = partition(data_subset,feat)
115             if
min(len(false_examples),len(true_examples))>=self.min_child_weight:
116                err = (self.sum_losses(false_examples)
117                       + self.sum_losses(true_examples))
118                self.display(3," split on",feat.__doc__,"has error=",err,
119                         "splits
into",len(true_examples),":",len(false_examples),"gamma=",self.gamma)
120                if err < best_error:
121                    best_feat = feat
122                    best_error=err
123                    best_partition = false_examples, true_examples
124         self.display(2,"best split is on",best_feat.__doc__,
125                              "with err=",best_error)
126         return best_feat, best_partition
127
128      def sum_losses(self, data_subset):
129         """returns sum of losses for dataset (with no more splits)
130         There a single prediction for all leaves using leaf_prediction
131         It is evaluated using loss
132         """
133         prediction = self.leaf_value(data_subset, self.target.frange)
134         error = sum(self.loss(prediction, self.target(e))
135                     for e in data_subset)
136         return error
137
138  def partition(data_subset,feature):
139      """partitions the data_subset by the feature"""
140      true_examples = []
141      false_examples = []
142      for example in data_subset:
143         if feature(example):
144             true_examples.append(example)
145         else:
146             false_examples.append(example)
147      return false_examples, true_examples

Exercise 7.8  The γ implemented here is the improvement in the sum of the errors.
Why  might  the  sum  be  preferred  to  the  avergage?   Why  might  the  average  be
preferred to the sum? (Hint: think about few examples versus many examples at a
node.) Which works better in practice? You will have to carry out hyperparameter
tuning foreach one separately.

Exercise 7.10   Without any input features, it is often better to include a pseudo-
count that is added to the counts from the training data. Test the code that includes
a pseudo-count for the predictions.   When evaluating a split,  including pseudo
counts can make the split worse than no split.  Does pruning with an improve-
ment bound (γ) and pseudo-counts make the algorithm work better than with an
improvement bound by itself?
Exercise 7.11    Some  people  have  suggested  using  information  gain  (which  is
equivalent  to  greedy  optimization  of  log  loss)  as  the  measure  of  improvement
when building the tree, even in they want to have non-probabilistic predictions
in the final tree.  Does this work better than myopically choosing the split that is
best for the evaluation criteria used to judge the final prediction?
7.6  k-fold Cross Validation and Parameter
Tuning
Torunthecrossvalidationdemo,infolder"aipython",
load"learnCrossValidation.py",usinge.g.,   ipython    -i
learnCrossValidation.py.The  commented-out  commands  at  the
bottom  can  produce  a  graph  like  Figure  7.15.Different  runs  will
produce  different  graphs,  so  your  graph  will  be  different  the  one  in
[Poole and Mackworth, 2023].
k-fold cross validation is more sophisticated than dividing the non-test set
into a training and validation set as done above.
The above decision tree algorithm tends to overfit the data. One way to de-
termine whether the prediction is overfitting is by cross validation.  The code
below  implements  k-fold  cross  validation,  which  can  be  used  to  choose  the
value of parameters to best fit the training data.   If we want to use parame-
ter tuning to improve predictions on a particular dataset, we can only use the
training data (and not the test data) to tune the parameter.
k-fold cross validation partitions the training set into k approximately equal-
sized folds.  For each fold, it trains on the other examples, and determine the
error of the prediction on that fold. For example, if there are 10 folds, it train on
90% of the data, and tests on remaining 10% of the data.  It does this 10 times,
so that each example gets used as a test set once, and in the training set 9 times.
The code below creates one copy of the data, and multiple views of the data.
For each fold,  fold enumerates the examples in the fold,  and fold_complement
enumerates the examples not in the fold.   To use k-fold cross validation,  set
prob_valid to 0 in Data, as this does its own division into validation sets.
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

1827.  Supervised Machine Learning
learnCrossValidation.py — Cross Validation for Parameter Tuning
11  from learnProblem import Data_set, Data_from_file, Loss
12  from learnNoInputs import Predict
13  from learnDT import DT_learner
14  import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
15  import random
16
17  class K_fold_dataset(object):
18      def __init__(self, training_set, num_folds):
19         self.data = training_set.train.copy()
20         self.target = training_set.target
21         self.input_features = training_set.input_features
22         self.num_folds = num_folds
23         self.conditions = training_set.conditions
24
25         random.shuffle(self.data)
26         self.fold_boundaries = [(len(self.data)*i)//num_folds
27                               for i in range(0,num_folds+1)]
28
29      def fold(self, fold_num):
30         for i in range(self.fold_boundaries[fold_num],
31                       self.fold_boundaries[fold_num+1]):
32             yield self.data[i]
33
34      def fold_complement(self, fold_num):
35         for i in range(0,self.fold_boundaries[fold_num]):
36             yield self.data[i]
37         for i in range(self.fold_boundaries[fold_num+1],len(self.data)):
38             yield self.data[i]
The validation error is the average error for each example, where we test on
each fold, and learn on the other folds.
learnCrossValidation.py — (continued)
40      def validation_error(self, learner, error_measure, **other_params):
41         error = 0
42         try:
43             for i in range(self.num_folds):
44                predictor = learner(self,
train=list(self.fold_complement(i)),
45                                  **other_params).learn()
46                error += sum( error_measure(predictor(e), self.target(e))
47                             for e in self.fold(i))
48         except ValueError:
49             return float("inf") #infinity
50         return error/len(self.data)
The plot_error method plots the average error as a function of the mini-
mum number of examples in decision-tree search, both for the validation set
and for the test set.   The error on the validation set can be used to tune the
parameter — choose the value of the parameter that minimizes the error.  The
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

7.6.  k-fold Cross Validation and Parameter Tuning183
error on the test set cannot be used to tune the parameters; if it were to be used
this way it could not be used to test how well the method works on unseen
examples.

52  def plot_error(data, criterion=Loss.squared,

54                   num_folds=5, maxx=None, xscale='linear'):
55      """Plots the error on the validation set and the test set
56      with respect to settings of the minimum number of examples.
57      xscale should be'log' or'linear'
58      """
59      plt.ion()
60      fig, ax = plt.subplots()
61      ax.set_xscale(xscale) # change between log and linear scale
62      ax.set_xlabel("min_child_weight")
63      ax.set_ylabel("average "+criterion.__doc__)
64      folded_data = K_fold_dataset(data, num_folds)
65      if maxx == None:
66         maxx = len(data.train)//2+1
67      verrors = []  # validation errors
68      terrors = []  # test set errors
69      for mcw in range(1,maxx):
70         verrors.append(folded_data.validation_error(DT_learner, criterion,
71                                                    leaf_prediction=leaf_prediction,
72                                                    min_child_weight=mcw))
73         tree = DT_learner(data, criterion, leaf_prediction=leaf_prediction,
74                             min_child_weight=mcw).learn()
75         terrors.append(data.evaluate_dataset(data.test,tree,criterion))
76      ax.plot(range(1,maxx), verrors, ls='-',color='k',
77                 label="validation for "+criterion.__doc__)
78      ax.plot(range(1,maxx), terrors, ls='--',color='k',
79                 label="test set for "+criterion.__doc__)
80      ax.legend()

7.7  Linear Regression and Classification
Here is a stochastic gradient descent searcher for linear regression and classifi-
cation.
learnLinear.py — Linear Regression and Classification
11  from learnProblem import Learner
12  import random, math
13
14  class Linear_learner(Learner):
15      """Linear Learner
16      """
17      title = "Linear learner"
18      def __init__(self, dataset, train=None,
19                 learning_rate=0.01, max_init = 0, squashed=None, bias0=None,
20                 num_iter = 1000):
21         """
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

7.7.  Linear Regression and Classification185
22         Creates a gradient descent searcher for a linear classifier.
23         The main learning is carried out by learn()
24
25         dataset provides the target and the input features
26         train a subset of the training data to use (None means use all
training examples)
27         learning_rate is the gradient descent step size
28         max_init is the maximum absolute value of the initial weights (does
this matter?)
29         squashed specifies whether the output is a sigmoid (None means
infer from dataset)
30         bias0 is initial bias if not None
31         num_iter is the default number of iterations
32         """
33         self.dataset = dataset
34         self.target = dataset.target
35         if train==None:
36             self.train = self.dataset.train
37         else:
38             self.train = train
39         self.learning_rate = learning_rate
40         if squashed is None:
41             self.squashed = dataset.target.ftype =='boolean'
42         else:
43             self.squashed = squashed
44         self.input_features = [one]+dataset.input_features # one is defined
below
45         self.weights = {feat:random.uniform(-max_init,max_init)
46                        for feat in self.input_features}
47         if bias0 is not None:
48             self.weights[one] = bias0
49         self.num_iter = num_iter
predictor predicts the value of an example from the current parameter set-
tings.
learnLinear.py — (continued)
51
52      def predictor(self,e):
53         """returns the prediction of the learner on example e"""
54         linpred = sum(w*f(e) for f,w in self.weights.items())
55         if self.squashed:
56             return sigmoid(linpred)
57         else:
58             return linpred
59
60      def __str__(self, sig_dig=3):
61         """returns the doc string for the current prediction function
62         sig_dig is the number of significant digits in the numbers"""
63         doc = "+".join(str(round(val,sig_dig))+"*"+feat.__doc__
64                        for feat,val in self.weights.items())
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

1867.  Supervised Machine Learning
65         if self.squashed:
66             return "sigmoid("+ doc+")"
67         else:
68             return doc
learn is the main algorithm of the learner.  It does num_iter steps (batches) of
stochastic gradient descent, for the given batch size.
learnLinear.py — (continued)
70      def learn(self, batch_size=32, num_iter=None):
71         if num_iter is None:
72             num_iter = self.num_iter
73         batch_size = min(batch_size, len(self.train))
74         d = {feat:0 for feat in self.weights}
75         for it in range(num_iter):
76             self.display(2,f"prediction= {self}")
77             for e in random.sample(self.train, batch_size):
78                error = self.predictor(e) - self.target(e)
79                for feat in self.weights:
80                    d[feat] += error*feat(e)
81             for feat in self.weights:
82                self.weights[feat] -= self.learning_rate*d[feat]
83                d[feat]=0
84         return self.predictor
one is a function that always returns 1.  This is used for the “bias” input prop-
erty.

learnLinear.py — (continued)
96  def softmax(xs, domain=None):
97      """xs is a list of values, and
98      domain is the domain (a list) or None if the list should be returned
99      returns a distribution over the domain (a dict)
100      """
101      m = max(xs) # use of m prevents overflow (and all values underflowing)
102      exps = [math.exp(x-m) for x in xs]
103      s = sum(exps)
104      if domain:
105         return {d:v/s for (d,v) in zip(domain,exps)}
106      else:
107         return [v/s for v in exps]
108
109  def indicator(v, domain):
110      return [1 if v==dv else 0 for dv in domain]

The following plots the prediction as a function of the number of steps of
gradient descent. We first define a version of range that allows for real numbers
(integers and floats). This is similar to numpy.arange.
learnLinear.py — (continued)
172  def arange(start,stop,step):
173      """enumerates values in the range [start,stop) separated by step.
174      like range(start,stop,step) but allows for integers and floats.
175      Rounding errors are expected with real numbers. (or use numpy.arange)
176      """
177      while start<stop:
178         yield start
179         start += step

7.8  Rating Data
Much real-world data does not come in the terms of tables where each tuple
is an independent example, the columns are features, and one of the columns
is to be learned as a function of the other columns.  Data typically has to be
transformed into such a format.
MovieLens
1
is non-commercial, personalized movie recommendation sys-
tem,  for  which  a  number  of  datasets  are  publicly  available.   The  items  are
movies  and  the  properties  are  genres  of  movies.   The  aim  here  is  to  predict
the rating of a movie for a user.  Other possible learning tasks are predicting
user demographic or movie information from ratings, or using external movie
database information to make even more informed predictions.
Each dataset consists of two files used here:
•  a  ratings  file  consisting  of  (userId,  movieId,  rating,  timestamp)  tuples,
where userId and movieId are integer identifiers, the rating is an integer
from 1 to 5, and the timestamp is the Unix time (the number of seconds
since January 1, 1970, 00:00:00 UTC) that the rating was entered
•  a movies file that gives the (movieId, title, genres).
(Other files provide user-provided tags for the movies, and links to movie data
at https://www.imdb.com and https://www.themoviedb.org, but these are not
used here. Older datasets include some demographic information about users.)
1
Movielens  [Harper  and  Konstan,  2015]  is  a  research  project  by  the  GroupLens  research
group at the University of Minnesota.   Datasets of various sizes are available from https://
grouplens.org/datasets/movielens/.
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

1927.  Supervised Machine Learning
There are 18 genres for movies, including action, adventure, animation, chil-
dren, comedy, and crime.  Each movie can have multiple genres.  For example,
Toy Story (1995) has genres animation, children, comedy. The rating is an integer
from 1 to 5.
These are easy-to-understand datasets, where students may have even watched
some of the movies. The aim of the learners below is to predict the future from
the past; ratings before a timestamp are used for training and those after are
used for testing. This is a more reasonable test of a real application than choos-
ing  random  splits.   The  training  datasets  have  a  diversity  of  the  number  of
movies rated by each user number of ratings per movie – including just one
rating  –  which also  is  closer  to  real-world applications  than  only  looking  at
people or movies with many ratings.
This domain is pedagogically interesting because there are multiple learn-
ing possibilities.  You can learn global predictions (using all of the ratings) or
treat  each  user  as  a  dataset.   It  is  challenging  for  a  number  of  reasons:  The
number of training ratings per user ranges from zero to hundreds.   The rat-
ings are not selected at random; people tend to only watch movies they think
they might like.  It os common for someone to not rate movies of all genres;
for example, many people have not rated horror movies, and thus it is difficult
to predict whether they will like horror movies.  Similar issues arise in many
practical datasets.
In the Movielens dataset(s), all users had rated at least 20 movies.  To keep
the data sets small, to create for a diversity of number of ratings per user, and
to allow multiple datasets from the same data,  only ratings between a start-
time and end-time are included. The test-train split is also by time: movies on
or before the given timestamp are in the training set, those after are in the test
set.  Different datasets are obtained by changing the timestamps.  This means
that some users have few movies in the training set, although we only include
users who have at least one..  This is more representative of a real application
where many have few ratings – indeed everyone starts with few ratings, and
presumably they need recommendations to keep them on the system.
The following assumes that the ml-32m dataset is downloaded from https:
//grouplens.org/datasets/movielens/. Unzip ml-32m.zip into the current di-
rectory.   This is large file,  for which only a few ratings are used at any time
(ratings between timestamps).  It is possible to use the older ml-100k dataset,
but this has a different format for the movie file, but also includes some user
demographic information.  The other small datasets have very few users with
ratings both before and after a given timestamp.
The first learners below use the given genres of movies as the input features.
There is a Boolean feaure for each genre, that is true of a rating when the movie
being rated has that genre.   In Section 7.9,  movies features are learned from
data.
The ratings are integers from 1 to 5. There are three variants of the learning
problem to predict ratings::
https://aipython.orgVersion 0.9.18September 18, 2026

7.8.  Rating Data193
•  A numeric prediction for a rating is a real value, such as 3.4, evaluated
using squared error.
•  A  categorical  prediction  is  a  probability  distribution  over  the  ratings,
such as {1:0.1, 2:0.1, 3:0.3, 4:0.3, 5:0.2}, evaluated using catgorical log loss.
•  A Boolean prediction is a probability of a proposition, by default that the
rating is greater than 3, such as 0.65, evaluated using binary log loss.
A |Ratings| object holds the training and test triples, and defines the input
features. These are not aipython Data_sets as the target type is not defined.
learnRatings.py — Rating data for linear learner

107                        self.item_props[movieId][self.genre2pos[gen]] = 1
108                    elif gen in self.extra_genres:

110                    else:
111                        self.extra_genres[gen] = 1
112             if self.extra_genres:
113                 self.display(1,f"extra genres: {self.extra_genres}")
114
115             self.show_stats()
116             return self

The preceding code gave global predictions:  the same rating for a movie
was given for each user.  The following code creates a dataset for each user.
It  only  constructs  datasets  for  users  with  some  (>  0)  training  ratings  and  a
minumum number of test ratings. A diverse number of training examples (in-
cluding very few) allows for the range of possible number to be explored.  In-
cluding only a minimum number of test is to reduce randomness due to which
test examples were included. (The statisically minded might like to determine
the confidince intervals for various sizes of min_num_test.)  Ensuring enough
test cases to average out random fluctuations might make the results biased as
the would only includes people who made multiple ratings in a short period,
which might not be representative of the population.

23      def __init__(self, ratings, min_num_test=10):
24         """ create a dataset for each user.

27         """

32             if user not in self.user2train:
33                self.user2train[user] = []
34             self.user2train[user].append((user,item,rating))
35         for (user,item,rating) in self.ratings.test:
36             if user in self.user2test:

38             else:

min_num_test:

ratings.item_props)

self.userRatings.items())}

139  def data2latex(header, data, ver=True, number_examples=True):
140      """create a LaTeX table.
141      header is a list
142      data is a list of lists, where each element is the same length as the
header.
143      We use ver defined in latex by \newcommand{\ver}[1]{\rotatebox{90}{#1}}
144      """
145      if number_examples:
146         header = ["Example \\#"]+header
147         data = [["$e_{"+str(i+1)+"}$"]+tuple for (i,tuple) in
enumerate(data)]
148      print("\\begin{tabular}{","l"*len(data[0]),"}", sep="")
149      if header:
150         if ver:
151             print(' &'.join("\\ver{"+str(e)+"}" for e in header),'\\\\')
152         else:
153             print(' &'.join(str(e) for e in header),'\\\\')
154      for row in data:
155         print(' &'.join(str(e) for e in row),'\\\\')
156      print("\\end{tabular}")
